# How to use this questionnaire

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr class="header">
<th><p><strong>Do not force certainty</strong></p>
<p>A complete plan does not require every design answer to be final. It requires every important uncertainty to be visible. When you do not know an answer, mark it as a test and state what prototype or playtest would decide it.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Decision-status tags

| **\[L\] Locked**      | A committed decision that other systems may depend on.         |
|-----------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **\[P\] Provisional** | Current preference; safe to plan around, but open to revision. |
| **\[T\] Test**        | Must be decided through a prototype, calculation, or playtest. |
| **\[U\] Unknown**     | Not yet considered; must be researched or discussed.           |
| **\[CUT\]**           | Explicitly outside the current scope.                          |
| **\[LATER\]**         | Relevant eventually, but not needed for the next milestone.    |

## How much to answer at each stage

Pass 1 - Core planning questions: Answer CORE-01 through CORE-55. This is enough to create the first production roadmap and identify the combat prototype.

Pass 2 - Prototype modules: Answer the combat, build, enemy, and technical questions needed for the first playable laboratory.

Pass 3 - Vertical-slice modules: Answer the equipment, loot, world, quest, economy, and UX questions required for one complete expedition.

Pass 4 - Full-production modules: Finalize content scale, narrative breadth, endgame, launch requirements, and production estimates only after the slice works.

## Response format

*Example: CORE-03 \[P\]: Explore a dense fantasy world, master projectile combat, and build specialized equipment combinations.*

*Example: COMBAT-08 \[T\]: Test a dash with 0.15 seconds of invulnerability against a faster dash with no invulnerability.*

## Contents

Part I - Core planning questions (CORE-01 to CORE-55)

Part II - Combat and controls

Part III - Weapons, skills, classes, and builds

Part IV - Levels, statistics, and progression

Part V - Equipment, loot, inventory, and economy

Part VI - Enemies, encounters, dungeons, and bosses

Part VII - World structure, exploration, and traversal

Part VIII - Quests, narrative, factions, and world recognition

Part IX - Crafting, gathering, hubs, settlements, and automation

Part X - Grind, rewards, repetition, and respect for time

Part XI - Death, saving, difficulty, and recovery

Part XII - Interface, accessibility, art, audio, and readability

Part XIII - Technical architecture, tools, and content pipeline

Part XIV - Production, milestones, testing, and risk

Part XV - Vertical-slice specification and planning extraction sheet

# Part I - Core planning questions

## A. Identity and player promise

**CORE-01. What is the current working title?**

Answer/status: \[P\] Wildshot Adventures

**CORE-02. Describe the game in one genre sentence.**

*Consider: Example structure: "A top-down 2D open-world projectile action RPG with MMO-style progression."*

Answer/status: \[P\] A top-down 2D open-world fantasy action RPG with freely aimed projectile combat, three permanently chosen classes, MMO-style leveling, class-specific skill trees, and tiered equipment progression.

**CORE-03. What is the one-sentence promise to the player?**

*Consider: State what the player repeatedly does, what they become, and what makes the experience distinctive.*

Answer/status: \[L\] Explore a vast fantasy world filled with battles and events as a permanently chosen Archer, Warrior, or Mage; master freely aimed projectile combat; gain levels and class skill points through quests, monsters, and other activities; and collect progressively stronger RPG equipment and class-specific weapons, including rare boss-dropped uniques whose powerful but situational projectile patterns create distinct ways to fight.

Supporting decisions: \[L\] A character chooses one class at creation and cannot change it. Each account or local profile supports at least three character slots. Weapons are class-specific and own the basic primary-attack projectile pattern. The skill tree improves the class combat plan without routinely replacing that pattern. Standard tiered weapons provide the dependable vertical power ladder; unique boss weapons provide powerful but situational patterns and require deliberate, targetable grinding. Direct pattern-changing skill-tree nodes are excluded from the baseline design.

**CORE-04. What fantasy should the player inhabit?**

*Consider: Examples: wandering adventurer, monster hunter, class specialist, faction champion, settlement protector, treasure seeker.*

Answer/status: \[L\] A rising-hero, zero-to-hero fantasy. The player begins as an unknown and inexperienced adventurer, then becomes a powerful hero whose achievements are increasingly recognized by the world.

Supporting direction: \[P\] Mechanical growth through levels, skill-tree development, equipment, and difficult victories should be reinforced by social and world recognition. The exact recognition systems will be decided in the quest, faction, settlement, and world-progression sections.

**CORE-05. What should the game feel like during ordinary play, dangerous encounters, and major victories?**

*Consider: Choose emotional targets such as relaxed, curious, tactical, tense, powerful, clever, rewarded, or triumphant.*

Answer/status: \[L\] Ordinary open-world exploration and normal-enemy grinding should feel laid-back, satisfying, and productive. Dungeons and world bosses should shift the experience into faster, more energetic, and more demanding combat. Appropriately leveled content must retain meaningful resistance as the player follows the natural progression curve.

Boss lifecycle: \[L\] Important endgame bosses should initially emphasize mastery. Their readable projectile patterns should take several attempts to learn and defeat efficiently. Once the player is comfortable with an encounter, the main motivation should shift toward efficient farming and the excitement of obtaining its unique rewards. Boss-access difficulty, encounter difficulty, unique usefulness, and drop rarity must be balanced as one combined system.

**CORE-06. What are the three or four non-negotiable design pillars?**

*Consider: Each pillar should describe a player experience, not a feature list.*

Answer/status: \[L\] Four non-negotiable design pillars:

1\. Freely aimed projectile combat is satisfying, readable, and expressive in its own right - From relaxed open-world grinding to demanding dungeons, bosses, and raids, movement, independent aiming, weapon attacks, positioning, dodging, enemy prioritization, and class skills should feel responsive and create meaningful decisions. Enemies, weapons, skills, and builds should provide varied ways to fight while preserving clear feedback and learnable threats. Not every encounter must require every action, but active control, execution, and game knowledge should consistently improve the player's safety and efficiency. Combat must remain enjoyable even when no valuable item drops.  
2. Meaningful progression continually expands who the player is, what they can do, and what they can pursue - The player grows from an unknown, inexperienced adventurer into a powerful, recognized hero through self-directed play. Progress should be tangible across character strength, combat possibilities, equipment and builds, knowledge, access, reputation, and the challenges the player can pursue and overcome—not merely through larger numbers. Across the journey, progression should regularly unlock new worthwhile grinds and long-term pursuits. As the player becomes stronger, more knowledgeable, better equipped, and more accomplished, they become capable of reaching, surviving, and efficiently farming new enemies, regions, portal sources, dungeons, bosses, raids, rewards, equipment, and build opportunities. Progression should therefore create new reasons to play, rather than only making old content faster. Different activities and playstyles may provide different routes forward. Earlier grinds may remain useful without preventing new ones from becoming increasingly ambitious and rewarding. Reaching endgame begins another substantial stage of progression through specialization, build refinement, targeted rewards, collection, mastery, and the game's hardest pursuits. New pursuits do not all require literal level gates; they may become available because the player has gained sufficient power, found access, completed a prerequisite, learned where to hunt, or developed enough skill to handle them.  
3. Exploration builds world knowledge, and world knowledge turns discovery into deliberate pursuits - The world should reward curiosity, attention, and accumulated experience. Through exploration and play, the player learns its regions, routes, enemies, dangers, prerequisites, portal sources, reward sources, and other meaningful relationships. This knowledge helps them choose concrete goals, prepare appropriately, and deliberately pursue new content, rewards, and grinds rather than depend on directionless wandering, forced waiting, or arbitrary luck just to begin an attempt. Discovery should provide both immediate and future value. The player may find secrets, landmarks, encounters, useful routes, or dangerous content before they are ready to overcome it, creating reasons to return later. As the player's goals, strength, access, and knowledge develop, earlier regions may gain new relevance instead of simply becoming exhausted and forgotten. Specific enemies dropping portals to associated dungeons remains an important expression of this pillar, but not its entire scope. Similar learnable and targetable relationships may connect the player to desired equipment, bosses, quests, regions, and other progression opportunities. Essential knowledge should eventually be discoverable or recordable inside the game. Mystery, uncertain rewards, and unexpected opportunities should remain, and roaming without a fixed goal should still be worthwhile. "Targetable" means that once the player understands a pursuit, they can normally attempt it deliberately; it does not guarantee the desired reward, remove travel or preparation, or reduce the world to selecting destinations from a menu.  
4. Mastering major challenges becomes a rewarding long-term pursuit - Dungeons, world bosses, raids, optional superbosses, and other concentrated encounters begin as dangerous but learnable challenges. As the player improves their knowledge, execution, build, and equipment, they become increasingly consistent and efficient to overcome. This creates satisfying repetition and long-term goals through character progression, stronger equipment, powerful situational unique items, completion goals, and greater mastery. Mastery does not necessarily make difficult content trivial; the hardest encounters may remain demanding even after the player understands them.  
Portal implementation direction: \[L/P\] Specific mobs are associated with specific dungeons and directly drop their portals; fragments are not the baseline. Normal-mob portal rates are currently envisioned around 20-30%, and associated world-boss rates around 50-100%, subject to testing. Relevant mobs must be available densely and reliably enough that knowledgeable, targeted hunting is practical rather than frustrating.

**CORE-07. What are the five strongest anti-pillars?**

*Consider: State what the game must not become, such as waiting-based MMO padding, unreadable bullet density, or limitless mandatory RNG.*

Answer/status: \[L\] Five anti-pillars:

1\. No passive or self-playing combat - Appropriately leveled enemies must not be defeated effectively by merely holding fire and ignoring their behavior. Active aiming, movement, awareness, and skill use must make the player noticeably safer and more efficient.  
2. No MMO-style waiting used as content - The game must not rely on daily or weekly lockouts, long forced respawns, rare-spawn camping, forced waiting, or excessive empty travel to extend playtime.  
3. No directionless or unnecessarily cluttered grind - Desired dungeons and rewards must have learnable, targetable sources. Fragments, currencies, conversion steps, and inventory items should be added only when they create a distinct decision or benefit rather than needless friction.  
4. No unreadable projectile combat - Combat may become relentlessly intense, especially in endgame content, but hostile attacks, hazards, safe spaces, and intended responses must remain visually legible and mechanically learnable. Deaths should be understandable rather than caused by obscured or indistinguishable effects.  
5. No universally superior unique weapon - A unique weapon may be exceptionally powerful in its intended situation, but it must retain meaningful tradeoffs and must not automatically invalidate every other weapon available to its class.

**CORE-08. Which three to five games are the closest references, and what exact element should be learned from each?**

*Consider: Separate combat, progression, world structure, loot, questing, interface, and presentation references.*

Answer/status: \[L\] Two primary references are intentionally locked for now. Additional references should be added only when they contribute a distinct missing lesson rather than to satisfy a quota. Reference 1: Realm of the Mad God - the primary experiential, combat-loop, dungeon-access, raid, and loot-chase reference. Wildshot Adventures should capture the feeling of independently aimed projectile combat, movement under pressure, class-specific weapons, tiered progression, and world-to-portal-to-dungeon-to-boss progression, adapted into a deliberately single-player-first game rather than copied feature for feature.

Realm of the Mad God - elements to learn: \[L\] (1) move and aim/fire independently; (2) readable projectile encounters that can escalate to extreme endgame intensity; (3) specific open-world enemies and world bosses dropping portals to associated dungeons; (4) class-specific weapon families whose patterns meaningfully change combat; (5) bosses that are initially learned and later farmed efficiently for desirable equipment; (6) long, very difficult solo raid-scale endgame dungeons that demand sustained execution, preparation, and boss knowledge; and (7) a clear reward split between broadly obtainable tiered equipment and authored unique loot.

Loot-source direction: \[L/P\] Tiered weapons and equipment form the dependable vertical power ladder and may drop from normal enemies, elites, world bosses, dungeon encounters, raid encounters, chests, and other suitable sources. The maximum tier follows the difficulty and progression level of the source. Bosses may drop tiered gear as a baseline reward in addition to their unique chance. Unique weapons and equipment are restricted to named, authored high-value sources: world bosses and dungeon or raid encounters. Selected major quests remain under consideration as guaranteed unique sources \[P\]; generic normal enemies do not randomly drop uniques.

Realm of the Mad God scope note: \[L/P\] It is a feeling and structural reference, not a blueprint. Wildshot Adventures should translate its tension, discovery, mastery, targeted portal hunting, tiered equipment progression, unique-item pursuit, dungeons, and raid-scale challenge into a single-player-first RPG with its own world, classes, balance, death rules, quests, and progression systems. Exact raid structure, checkpoints, entry rules, rewards, class filtering, drop rates, duplicate handling, and quest-unique rules will be decided later.

Reference 2: \[L\] Erenshor - the primary reference for the long-form single-player-MMO journey. Wildshot Adventures should capture the experience of entering a substantial fantasy world with almost nothing, navigating dense zones full of quests, enemies, dungeons, secrets, and equipment goals, and gradually becoming capable of defeating creatures and entering places that once seemed impossible.

Erenshor - elements to learn: \[L\] (1) slow, meaningful zero-to-hero character growth; (2) MMO-scale progression that works entirely in single player and on the player's own schedule; (3) dense, content-rich maps with many goals, discoveries, and reasons to revisit; (4) targetable equipment hunting and substantial repetitive grind that feels worthwhile because it moves the player toward the next zone or challenge; (5) world and systems knowledge as a real form of progression, including stats, builds, routes, enemies, loot sources, quests, and encounter rules; (6) difficult solo dungeons and raids with gradual endgame gearing; (7) relaxed repetition as a valid and enjoyable mode of play; and (8) quests, lore, factions, and atmosphere that give context to self-directed farming and exploration.

Reference-pair synthesis: \[L\] Realm of the Mad God supplies the kinetic combat language, projectile-pattern mastery, targeted portal hunting, dungeons, raids, and unique-loot chase. Erenshor supplies the slower structure around that combat: a world worth inhabiting for a long time, a difficult knowledge-driven climb from nothing, heavy but purposeful repetition, tangible equipment growth, meaningful return visits, and end content that requires broad understanding of the game rather than raw reflexes alone. SimPlayers, party simulation, tab-target combat, and other Erenshor-specific implementations are not automatically desired features.

Player-experience context: \[CONTEXT\] Reported playtime is approximately 2,580.2 hours in Realm of the Mad God, 333 hours in Erenshor, 2,189.2 hours in the now-inactive MMO Damascus, and 1,202.7 hours in Tales of Yore. Damascus and Tales of Yore are preference signals only, not formal references at this stage. This strongly cautions against equating respect for the player's time with removing grind: the intended standard is grind that is deliberate, learnable, targetable, cumulative, and rewarding enough that a player may willingly repeat it for hundreds of hours. The two formal references should not limit later design ideas; they establish the strongest current experiential anchors.

**CORE-09. Which elements from those references should deliberately be avoided?**

*Consider: Name concrete systems or failure modes rather than saying only "do it better."*

Answer/status: \[L\] Wildshot Adventures deliberately borrows the strongest experiential lessons from Realm of the Mad God and Erenshor while rejecting the systems that would undermine a solo-first, knowledge-driven, projectile-action RPG. The concrete exclusions for both references are locked below.

Realm of the Mad God - elements to avoid: \[L\]  
1. No permanent character deletion as the normal death rule - failure must not erase the character, levels, or equipped build.  
2. No multiplayer dependence - every quest, dungeon, world boss, and raid must remain fully completable solo. Optional duo co-op may share the experience but cannot be required.  
3. No large-group zerging - encounters must be won through readable execution and progression rather than overwhelming them with player count.  
4. No trading-driven balance - important equipment must be personally obtainable, and rarity must not assume access to a player economy.  
5. No live-service pressure - no mandatory daily or weekly schedules, fear-of-missing-out progression, or limited-time obligations used to retain players.  
6. No uncontrolled visual clutter - player attacks, effects, and optional co-op visuals must not obscure hostile patterns or the player hitbox.  
7. No excessive external-wiki dependence - mystery and discovery are welcome, but essential knowledge about portals, loot sources, statistics, quests, and systems should become learnable or recordable inside the game.  
8. No excessive rebuilding after failure - difficult encounters may impose a meaningful penalty, but a failed dungeon or raid attempt must not require rebuilding an entire character or repeating an unreasonable amount of solved progression.  
Deferred details: \[P\] The exact death penalty, recovery rules, in-game knowledge tools, and co-op encounter scaling will be decided in their dedicated sections.

Erenshor - elements to avoid: \[L\]  
1. No SimPlayer or artificial-party system as a core requirement - the world should feel inhabited through authored NPCs, settlements, factions, quests, enemies, and optional real duo co-op rather than requiring a simulated MMO population.  
2. No tab-target or passive auto-attack combat - Wildshot Adventures borrows Erenshor's long-form journey, knowledge demands, equipment progression, and world density while retaining freely aimed projectile combat.  
3. No mandatory party-role composition - Archer, Warrior, and Mage must each be able to complete the entire game solo without requiring an AI tank, healer, or damage dealer.  
4. No waiting for named enemies or important progression - targetable farming should involve routing, fighting, and learning rather than standing still for long respawn timers.  
5. No obscurity without an in-game learning path - mysteries and limited initial knowledge are welcome, but essential statistics, loot sources, portal associations, quest clues, and progression requirements should eventually become discoverable or recordable inside the game.  
6. No progression where statistics completely replace execution - levels, gear, and system knowledge should matter greatly, but aiming, dodging, positioning, skill use, and boss-pattern understanding must remain relevant, especially in endgame dungeons and raids.  
7. No unnecessary inventory burden from situational materials - currencies, fragments, quest objects, and crafting materials should exist only when they create a distinct purpose or decision, not merely to imitate MMO complexity.

Preserved Erenshor qualities: \[L\] These exclusions do not reject substantial grind, slow and meaningful progression, a dangerous early world, incomplete initial knowledge, dense regions, named-enemy hunting, or repeated returns to earlier zones. Those are intentional parts of the desired experience when the repetition remains targetable, cumulative, and valuable.

**CORE-10. What is the clearest reason a player would choose this game over its closest alternatives?**

*Consider: This can be a combination of systems, but it must be understandable in one or two sentences.*

Answer/status: [L] Wildshot Adventures combines the long-term progression, dense world, meaningful grind, and systems knowledge of a classic MMO with freely aimed projectile combat in a complete single-player adventure. Players hunt specific enemies to earn dungeon access, master increasingly intense bosses and raids, and pursue tiered equipment and powerful situational uniques while rising from nothing into a legendary hero.

Short pitch: [L] A single-player MMO-scale adventure where every fight is freely aimed: hunt monsters for dungeon portals, master brutal projectile bosses and raids, and grind toward powerful equipment in a dense fantasy world.

Supporting play-mode direction: [L/P] Mouse and keyboard defines the intended combat experience and full design ceiling. Controller support may provide a convenient secondary option for relaxed exploration, grinding, couch play, and Steam Deck use, but it must not reduce encounter complexity, projectile intensity, precision demands, or endgame scope. If controller support would require weakening the intended game, controller support should be reduced or dropped rather than changing the content.

## B. Audience, format, and constraints

**CORE-11. Who is the primary player?**

*Consider: Describe preferred genres, desired complexity, mechanical skill, tolerance for grind, and likely play habits.*

Answer/status: [L] Wildshot Adventures is primarily aimed at experienced RPG, MMO, action-RPG, and projectile-combat players who enjoy deep systems, substantial purposeful grinding, long-term character progression, equipment hunting, and demanding endgame content. The opening should be understandable and reasonably forgiving, with difficulty and complexity increasing naturally as the player progresses. New players are welcome, but advanced systems, dungeons, raids, and endgame encounters will not be restricted or simplified solely to accommodate inexperienced players.

Design shorthand: [L] Accessible beginning, uncompromised endgame. Early accessibility should come from gradual introduction, clear feedback, readable attacks, and room to learn—not from lowering the game's eventual complexity or difficulty ceiling.

**CORE-12. What platforms are targeted first?**

*Consider: PC, Steam Deck, consoles, mobile, or another platform. State which is the lead platform.*

Answer/status: [L] Windows PC through Steam is the lead platform. Steam Deck compatibility is an intended target. Console releases may be considered later, but console certification, console-specific interfaces, and multi-platform requirements will not drive the initial scope or production plan.

**CORE-13. What are the primary input methods?**

*Consider: Mouse and keyboard, controller, touch, or several. State which must feel best.*

Answer/status: [L] Mouse and keyboard is the primary input method and defines the intended combat experience, precision ceiling, encounter complexity, and full endgame scope. Controller support is a secondary convenience for relaxed open-world grinding, exploration, couch play, and possible Steam Deck use. Controller support must never force reductions in projectile intensity, aiming demands, skill design, encounter scope, or endgame difficulty. If supporting controller properly would require compromising the intended game, controller support should be reduced or dropped rather than the game being weakened.

Supporting direction: [P/T] Controller controls, UI navigation, aim assistance, and input hot-swapping should be prototyped, but parity with mouse and keyboard is not a non-negotiable requirement.

**CORE-14. Is the game strictly single-player, optionally cooperative, or designed for multiplayer?**

*Consider: Treat networking as a foundational scope decision, not a feature that can be casually added later.*

Answer/status: \[P/T\] Wildshot Adventures is designed and balanced as a complete single-player game. Its technical architecture should support optional two-player online co-op, but co-op is not a promised launch feature until an early networked-combat prototype passes. Every class, quest, dungeon, raid, boss, unique item, and progression path must remain fully available and balanced for solo play. Co-op lets two friends share the same adventure rather than defining the experience. Four-player and mandatory group content are outside the current direction. This direction is conceptually confirmed; the \[T\] status remains because shipping co-op still depends on the early technical prototype.

Implementation direction: \[P/T\] Build core systems without assuming exactly one player: separate input from player simulation; give players and synchronized entities stable IDs and serializable state; use a host-authoritative model for enemies, projectiles, damage, loot, portals, dungeon state, and world state; keep character saves and personal loot per-player; write encounter logic to accept one or two players; and retain solo balance as the baseline with a separate co-op scaling layer. Run an early two-player greybox gate covering movement, aiming, shooting, one enemy, personal loot, shared portal entry, dungeon instancing, disconnect/reconnect behavior, and save safety. If that gate fails or harms the project, co-op can be cut without redesigning the solo game. Local or shared-screen co-op is a separate feature and is not implied by this decision.

**CORE-15. What is the business and ownership model?**

*Consider: Premium purchase, demo, early access, downloadable expansions, or another model. State whether any online account is required.*

Answer/status: [L] Wildshot Adventures is intended as a premium, one-time-purchase game on Steam. It will not be built around a subscription, battle pass, pay-to-win purchases, mandatory recurring payments, or live-service retention pressure.

Supporting direction: [P] The developer may also release the game for free on itch.io at their discretion, but Steam remains the primary commercial platform and the production plan should be built around a paid Steam release. A demo, Early Access, downloadable expansions, and any optional online-account requirements remain undecided until later production and release-planning questions.

**CORE-16. What is the desired normal session length?**

*Consider: Consider brief sessions, ordinary evenings, and long dungeon or boss sessions.*

**Answer/status:** [L] Wildshot Adventures does not prescribe one correct or normal session duration. A satisfying session is defined mainly by whether the player can actively pursue a self-chosen goal they genuinely care about, not by whether the game guarantees a permanent upgrade within a fixed amount of time. Brief and extended sessions should both be worthwhile, and CORE-16 does not lock numerical session-duration ranges.

**Meaningful session outcomes:** [L] A session may feel worthwhile through completing or advancing quests; exploring and discovering useful information; learning routes, enemies, mechanics, dungeons, or bosses; improving execution, knowledge, or clear efficiency; completing dungeon or boss attempts; performing additional targeted reward rolls; or receiving equipment, uniques, or other permanent progression when the rolls succeed. Receiving no unique item or permanent upgrade does not inherently make the session unsuccessful.

**Targeted RNG rule:** [L] Repeated targeted attempts use independent random rolls. More kills or clears create more total opportunities to succeed across the full pursuit, but a failed attempt does not improve the next attempt's probability, accumulate an escalating drop-rate modifier, or bank a pity value. A player may reasonably consider two unsuccessful boss kills meaningful progress because two relevant attempts were completed, even though the desired item is not mechanically closer and the next roll remains unchanged.

**Pause and ordinary-dungeon commitment:** [L] Active single-player gameplay should be pausable so real-life interruptions do not destroy an active run. An ordinary dungeon is normally one committed gameplay instance: pausing while remaining in the run is allowed, but leaving or abandoning ends that instance. The player cannot later resume the same partially cleared dungeon or permanently complete it room by room across separate entries.

**Raid scope:** [LATER] Exact raid duration, maximum uninterrupted commitment, wing structure, section boundaries, checkpoints, and continuation rules are deferred until raids are designed. Separate wings or sections remain possible design space, not a committed format.

**CORE-17. What is the target length of the first character's journey to endgame?**

*Consider: Avoid treating this as a fixed linear campaign. Separate focused progression, optional content, the threshold for reaching endgame, and later endgame time.*

**Answer/status:** [U] Open. Use **first character's journey to endgame** or **initial zero-to-hero journey**, not **first campaign**. The player may advance through different combinations of quests, ordinary enemy grinding, exploration, equipment hunting, dungeons, bosses, and other progression activities established later. No numerical duration, exact mechanical endgame threshold, or focused-versus-optional measurement method is locked.

**CORE-18. What replay or endgame experience is intended?**

*Consider: New builds, harder dungeons, optional bosses, world modifiers, collection, New Game Plus, or none.*

**Answer/status:** [U with locked constraints] The exact replay and endgame structure remains open. CORE-24 locks the broader direction that reaching endgame is a major transition rather than the end of the journey, and that endgame is a core part of Wildshot Adventures. Locked long-term pursuits include targeted farming, desired unique-item hunts, powerful and alternative builds, mastery of the hardest solo content, optional superbosses, collection and completion goals, and increasingly efficient play. The exact structure, sequence, systems, content volume, New Game Plus policy, world modifiers, and other replay rules remain unresolved.

**CORE-19. What tone, age rating, and content boundaries are intended?**

*Consider: Violence, horror, language, humor, themes, and visual intensity can affect design and production.*

**Answer/status:** [U] Unanswered.

**CORE-20. What are the hard production constraints?**

*Consider: Team size, skills, engine, budget, weekly hours, target timeframe, asset limitations, and platform requirements.*

**Answer/status:** [U] Unanswered.

## C. Loops, world, and long-term structure

**CORE-21. What is the ten-second gameplay loop?**

*Consider: Describe actions, decisions, feedback, and immediate result.*

**Answer/status:** [L] Encounter an ordinary enemy or group → initiate combat, or respond if an aggressive enemy attacks first → fight by attacking and using movement, positioning, dodging, enemy prioritization, and skills whenever the situation calls for them → repeat this combat exchange until the encounter is resolved → defeat the enemies → receive clear kill feedback and collect any loot or portal drops.

**Timing and variation:** [L] Ten seconds is only a representative window for the repeated moment-to-moment gameplay; it is not a required encounter duration. A fight may end quickly or continue through many repetitions of the combat exchange, depending on the enemies, encounter size, player strength, and circumstances. Not every fight requires every listed action: weaker enemies may be defeated directly, while more demanding encounters require more active movement, dodging, positioning, enemy prioritization, and skill use.

**Scope:** [L] CORE-21 describes ordinary, level-appropriate open-world combat. Boss encounters are outside this answer and may use longer, more structured, encounter-specific loops.

**CORE-22. What is the ten-minute activity loop?**

*Consider: Describe choosing an objective, traveling, fighting, discovering, and receiving progress.*

**Answer/status:** [L] Over roughly ten minutes, the player may pursue a specific self-chosen objective or simply roam and let opportunities emerge. They might collect or advance quests, perform relaxed repetitive enemy grinding for a particular form of progress, explore unfamiliar places, hunt a known reward or dungeon-portal source, enter or progress through a dungeon, pursue an encounter, or combine several of these activities.

**Activity flow:** [L] Travel, combat, discovery, quests, and rewards should flow into one another naturally. An unexpected quest, enemy, portal, item drop, landmark, or discovery may reinforce the current objective or cause the player to pursue something else.

**Progress and duration:** [L] The player should finish the representative window having made some relevant progress, such as XP, loot, quest advancement, discovery, knowledge, access, practice, improved efficiency, or additional targeted reward attempts. Ten minutes does not guarantee completion, a permanent upgrade, or an entire dungeon clear. Longer activities and pursuits may continue through many such windows.

**Low-attention grinding:** [L] Relaxed or "braindead" grinding means low-attention play that still requires active player control and fighting. It does not permit passive or self-playing combat.

**CORE-23. What is the one-hour progression loop?**

*Consider: Describe build preparation, expedition or dungeon completion, upgrades, world change, and next objective.*

**Answer/status:** [L] Over roughly one hour, the player may pursue one sustained self-chosen objective or combine several shorter activities. They might spend the whole period grinding particular enemies for XP, equipment, dungeon portals, or another targeted reward; advance one or more quests; explore a region; complete dungeon or boss attempts; or move naturally between these activities as new opportunities appear.

**Adaptation:** [L] When useful, the player may prepare or adjust equipment, skills, inventory, route, or target between activities, but this is not a mandatory ritual for every hour of play. A quest, discovery, portal, item drop, difficult encounter, or newly learned information may reinforce the current objective or lead into a different one.

**Meaningful progress:** [L] By the end of the representative hour, the player should normally have made meaningful progress toward something they chose: gaining XP or levels, developing the character, advancing quests, obtaining equipment, completing relevant loot attempts, clearing content, discovering locations or access routes, or improving knowledge, mastery, and efficiency.

**Duration and RNG:** [L] An hour does not guarantee a permanent upgrade, completed dungeon, world change, or finished objective. The entire hour may represent one complete pursuit, several connected activities, or only part of a much longer grind. Failed targeted loot attempts remain independent but still count as completed attempts toward the broader pursuit.

**CORE-24. What is the player's main long-term objective?**

*Consider: It may be narrative, territorial, factional, exploratory, or build-oriented.*

**Answer/status:** [L] The player's main long-term objective is to develop a chosen character from an unknown, inexperienced adventurer into a powerful, recognized, legendary endgame hero through levels, class development, equipment, exploration, quests, targeted grinding, increasingly difficult zones, dungeons, bosses, and raid-scale encounters.

**Endgame continuation:** [L] Reaching endgame is a major transition, not the end of the journey. A substantial, gradually progressing endgame continues through targeted farming, desired unique-item hunts, powerful and alternative builds, mastery of the hardest solo content, optional superbosses, collection, completion goals, and increasingly efficient play.

**Story boundary:** [L] Any eventual story conclusion or final threat may provide direction and an important milestone, but it is not the game's ultimate endpoint. The endgame must be a core part of Wildshot Adventures, not merely optional cleanup after the "real game" has ended.

**CORE-25. Which side activities are pillars, which are supporting systems, and which are excluded?**

*Consider: Examples: crafting, gathering, housing, settlement growth, collecting, professions, fishing, companions.*

**Answer/status:** [L] The four established design pillars remain Wildshot Adventures' only pillars. Non-combat activities are optional supporting systems intended to enrich the open world, provide relaxed alternative grinds, and create additional collection goals without competing with the main combat game.

**Accepted supporting systems:** [L] Foraging, fishing, and limited crafting are optional supporting systems, not design pillars or major focuses. Foraging and fishing make the vast open world feel richer, livelier, and more worth exploring while providing relaxed alternatives to combat.

**Progression and collection:** [L/P] Foraging has its own progression level and a long-term collection grind for uncommon and rare flowers. Fishing likewise has its own progression level and a rare-fish collection grind. Rewards from both should be primarily cosmetic and collection-oriented. Exact progression curves, rarity structures, collection interfaces, and reward sets remain unresolved.

**Collection presentation:** [L/P] Collectables should primarily be tracked, presented, and enjoyed through dedicated, polished interface menus rather than physical housing displays. Fishing, foraging, trophies and major accomplishments, cosmetics and skins, mounts, and future collection categories should contribute to an organized long-term collection system with clear progress and satisfying completion feedback. The detailed menu structure, metadata, previews, and presentation remain unresolved.

**Limited crafting:** [L/P] Crafting is closer to buying or commissioning a known reward through an in-world crafting presentation than to a broad profession. The player fulfills explicit costs or requirements and receives the selected, deterministic result. Foraging and fishing may supply resources or fulfill crafting requirements, allowing their optional gathering and collection grinds to lead toward known rewards. Crafting may support high-dedication optional rewards such as prestigious non-combat mounts, character or equipment skins, cosmetics, collection rewards, and other carefully selected non-combat reward types added later. It should remain extensible within this boundary without becoming a second complete equipment economy. Exact recipes, requirements, resource sources, interfaces, and eligible non-combat reward types remain unresolved.

**Boundary:** [L] Foraging and fishing should not be required for core progression, become mandatory combat-power grinds or daily chores, or create excessive material clutter. Regional flowers, fish, and suitable gathering locations may create discoveries and reasons to revisit the world. Crafting produces only non-combat rewards: it cannot create, improve, or reroll combat equipment; grant combat statistics or power; or produce combat consumables or buffs. Limited crafting should avoid a sprawling material economy, recipe clutter, random-output crafting, mandatory upkeep, and routine crafting chores.

**Housing classification:** [L] Player housing is not currently planned. It is not required for collection, storage, crafting, or progression and should not be added merely to provide somewhere to display rewards. This is not a permanent prohibition: housing may be reconsidered later only if a separate compelling purpose emerges.

**Settlement classification:** [L] Player-managed settlement growth is not planned. Settlements remain authored locations rather than player-built or managed systems. Selected settlements may undergo authored changes through quests, major victories, or world events. This is a supporting world and narrative system, not a design pillar or separate management activity.

**Pet classification:** [L/P] Pets are a small optional supporting system. One small pet may follow the player and provide a modest passive benefit, such as light healing, regeneration, or a small statistic or utility bonus. Pets do not attack, draw aggro, tank enemies, cast abilities, or function as AI party members, and they should complement the character rather than become a major source of combat power. Collection and appearance may be more important than maximizing pet power. Exact acquisition, progression, benefits, balance, and presentation remain unresolved.

**Wider-profession boundary:** [L] Fishing and foraging are the only non-combat activities currently planned with their own progression levels. Limited crafting is a deterministic reward system, not a leveled profession. No wider profession roster—such as mining, smithing, cooking, alchemy, or woodcutting—is currently planned. Another activity should be considered later only if it creates a genuinely distinct and valuable pursuit that fishing or foraging cannot already provide. The project prefers two strong non-combat activity systems over ten merely adequate ones.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact fishing, foraging, crafting, collection-menu, pet, and authored-settlement-change mechanics remain for their dedicated questions. CORE-25 classifies their roles and boundaries; it does not pre-design those later systems.

**CORE-26. How open is the world?**

*Consider: One seamless map, connected regions, open zones, hub and spokes, or another structure.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] Wildshot Adventures uses a layered multi-map world consisting of many large, distinct outdoor zone maps rather than one technically seamless map. Each zone is a substantial, freely explorable space belonging to the same coherent world.

**Zone connections:** [L] The player travels between neighbouring outdoor zones by physically finding and crossing their connecting roads, gates, mountain passes, tunnels, paths, or other entrances. Some connections are huge, obvious roads forming the world's main travel network, while others may be smaller, concealed, unusual, or discovered through exploration. Learning the routes and entrances is part of learning the world.

**Interior maps:** [L] Houses, shops, caves, ruins, dungeons, raids, and similar locations may use separate interior maps. Larger locations can contain multiple connected maps or deeper floors, and leaving normally returns the player to the entrance used.

**World-cohesion boundary:** [L] Map transitions and loading screens are acceptable, but travelling should feel like movement through an interconnected physical world rather than selection of disconnected levels from an overworld screen or menu.

**Auto-travel foundation:** [L/P] As the player progresses, they can unlock a convenient auto-travel system for selected previously discovered destinations. Auto-travel supplements physical exploration rather than replacing it: the player must initially discover the world, and unknown locations, hidden connections, and undiscovered routes cannot simply be selected through auto-travel.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact zone sizes, route placement, secret connections, and broader progression requirements remain unresolved. Procedural assembly is addressed by CORE-27, world gates by CORE-28, scaling by CORE-29, and detailed travel compression by CORE-30.

**CORE-27. How much of the world is handcrafted versus procedurally assembled?**

*Consider: State what procedural generation would accomplish beyond increasing size.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] Wildshot Adventures has a handcrafted world foundation with controlled procedural variation inside authored spaces.

**Authored foundation:** [L] Zone geography, roads, entrances, settlements, landmarks, caves, secrets, major interiors, bosses, progression-critical content, and the defining identity of major dungeons and raids are authored.

**Controlled variation:** [L/P] Procedural systems may vary enemy groups, roaming encounters, rare spawns, events, resource locations, fishing and foraging opportunities, and other repeatable details within authored spaces. Selected dungeons may eventually use curated room or encounter variations, but major dungeons and raids must retain authored identity, controlled mechanics, and learnable structure.

**Purpose and boundary:** [L] Procedural variation exists to keep revisiting and grinding familiar regions interesting, not merely to inflate world size. Randomness must not erase useful world knowledge, make targeted pursuits unreliable, constantly relocate essential destinations, or replace distinctive authored content with interchangeable filler.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact procedural systems, content pools, placement rules, reset schedules, dungeon applications, and the balance between persistence and variation remain unresolved for dedicated questions and prototypes.

**CORE-28. What gates progression through the world?**

*Consider: Levels, bosses, faction rank, keys, traversal abilities, story events, environmental protection, or soft danger.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] Wildshot Adventures uses mostly soft world gating, supported by selected meaningful hard gates.

**Outdoor access:** [L] Outdoor zones are not level-locked. Players may physically travel into dangerous regions early, explore cautiously, encounter powerful enemies, learn routes, discover portal sources and entrances, and see challenges they are not yet ready to overcome. Enemy danger is the main natural readiness barrier and should create visible future goals.

**Contained hard gates:** [L/P] Outdoor openness does not mean every location inside a zone is immediately accessible. Sealed ruins, inner areas, special routes, and similar contained spaces may use meaningful hard gates while the surrounding outdoor zone remains explorable.

**Instanced minimum levels:** [L/P] Dungeons, raids, and selected challenge instances use explicit minimum-level entry requirements. Their surrounding outdoor zones remain accessible regardless of level. If entry is attempted too early, the required level must be communicated clearly.

**Readiness boundary:** [L] Meeting the minimum level only grants permission to attempt the content; it does not guarantee success. Equipment, build quality, preparation, player skill, and knowledge remain important.

**Additional prerequisites:** [L/P] Selected content may additionally require a dropped portal, key, completed quest, boss victory, world event, environmental protection, discovered route, or another meaningful prerequisite.

**Requirement stacking and cost:** [L] Multiple access requirements may coexist only when each serves a clear purpose. They must not be stacked merely to extend playtime, and the total effort required to begin and repeat an attempt should remain proportionate to the content. Requirements must be clear before an access resource is spent; a portal, key, or similar item is never consumed when entry is denied.

**Persistence and repetition:** [L/P] Permanent progression gates normally remain solved once completed. Explicitly repeatable access items, such as dungeon portals, may still be required for each new attempt.

**World-flow boundary:** [L] Hard gates should be understandable and purposeful. The world should provide multiple viable frontiers where appropriate rather than forcing every player through one rigid universal sequence.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact minimum levels, which activities use them, and the placement and implementation of other gates remain unresolved for later progression and content planning.

**CORE-29. How does enemy and region scaling work?**

*Consider: Stable levels, soft bands, partial scaling, optional veteran versions, or universal scaling.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] Wildshot Adventures uses stable, authored difficulty. Nothing automatically scales to match the player's level, equipment, statistics, or progression.

**Regional structure:** [L] Each outdoor region has an intended difficulty range while still allowing easier outskirts, tougher inner areas, dangerous pockets, elites, rare enemies, and world bosses with their own fixed strength.

**Progression contrast:** [L] Entering a late region early should feel genuinely dangerous. Returning to an early region later should let the player feel substantially stronger and defeat its ordinary enemies efficiently.

**Outdoor and world-boss boundary:** [L] World bosses, outdoor enemies, and regions do not scale. Stronger later-game enemies should be distinct creatures, recognizable variants, or deliberately placed encounters rather than secretly scaled copies.

**Earlier-region relevance:** [L] Earlier regions remain worthwhile through quests, dungeon-portal sources, collections, fishing, foraging, events, secrets, rare spawns, and other targeted pursuits—not by raising every ordinary enemy to the player's level.

**Optional instanced versions:** [P] Selected instanced dungeons may later receive optional higher-difficulty versions. These are separately chosen versions with their own fixed enemy strength, mechanics, entry requirements, and rewards. They never adapt dynamically to whoever enters, and the original dungeon version remains unchanged.

**New-content boundary:** [L] Entirely new and stronger world bosses may be added later, but each individual world-boss encounter retains its authored difficulty rather than following the player.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact regional difficulty ranges, higher-difficulty dungeon-version names, eligibility, unlock conditions, mechanics, entry requirements, and rewards remain unresolved for later dungeon and progression planning.

**CORE-30. How will repeated travel and solved friction be compressed?**

*Consider: Shortcuts, fast travel, recall, mounts, cleared routes, automation, remote turn-in, or storage access.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] Wildshot Adventures uses a selective auto-travel system that functions as teleportation. It complements physical exploration without replacing the need to learn the world.

**Destination network:** [L/P] Auto-travel is available only to selected unlocked destinations, such as cities, settlements, major hubs, and appropriate landmarks. Discovering a location does not automatically make it an auto-travel destination.

**Manual-route boundary:** [L] Many caves, wilderness locations, secrets, and selected dungeons deliberately have no direct auto-travel access. The player teleports to the nearest suitable destination and manually follows the roads, concealed paths, or special entrances they have learned.

**Permanent unlocks:** [L/P] Eligible destinations may have different permanent unlock requirements, including discovering and activating the location, paying an initial cost, completing a quest, repairing a route, or meeting another appropriate condition.

**Activation boundary:** [L] Auto-travel may begin from most safe outdoor locations. It cannot begin during combat, near immediate danger, or inside houses, caves, dungeons, raids, and other contained maps.

**Cast and interruption:** [L] Teleportation has a visible three-to-five-second cast time. Moving, taking damage, or entering combat cancels the cast, preventing auto-travel from becoming an emergency escape.

**Currency cost:** [L/P] Each successful teleport costs currency. Currency is charged only when the teleport completes; a canceled or interrupted cast costs nothing. The fee should make teleportation a modest decision without forcing unnecessary walking.

**Travel completion:** [L] Once the cast completes, the player travels directly to a fixed safe arrival point. The route is not simulated, and no ambushes or travel interruptions occur during the teleport.

**Recall boundary:** [L] There is no separate free recall ability. Cities and hubs are ordinary auto-travel destinations using the same unlock rules, currency cost, and interruptible cast.

**Manual-travel support:** [P] Mounts, permanent shortcuts, and learned routes may make manual travel outside the teleport network faster. Their detailed mechanics belong to later questions.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact eligible destinations, individual unlock requirements, prices, whether costs vary by distance or destination, and detailed manual-travel aids remain unresolved for later world and economy planning. CORE-30 does not decide remote quest turn-in or remote storage access.

## D. Combat, builds, and progression

**CORE-31. What is the exact combat format?**

*Consider: Top-down free aim, twin-stick, projectile action, light bullet hell, focus targeting, pause options, and intended pace.*

**Answer/status:** [L] Wildshot Adventures uses real-time, top-down, freely aimed projectile-action combat. Keyboard movement and mouse aiming are independent. Attacks follow the player's aim rather than a selected or locked target.

**Seamless combat:** [L] Combat occurs in the current outdoor, interior, dungeon, or raid map. There is no separate battle screen, combat instance, turn transition, or required target-engagement state before the player can attack.

**No required target:** [L] Combat never requires a selected or locked target. The player can aim, fire, and activate combat actions without an enemy being present or selected, including aiming or firing into empty space wherever the action permits. Acquiring a target is never a prerequisite for attacking, using skills, dealing damage to an enemy, or killing it.

**Focus-target boundary:** [L/U] Any later focus-targeting feature must remain optional and subordinate to free aiming. It cannot become a prerequisite for any combat action. Its exact informational, controller-assistance, or other functions remain unresolved for CORE-35.

**Excluded formats and input boundary:** [L] There is no tab targeting, passive auto-combat, turn-based play, or tactical pause-and-command system. Controller support may use a secondary twin-stick equivalent, but it cannot restrict the primary mouse-and-keyboard combat design.

**Spatial resolution:** [L] An attack must physically connect with an enemy or other valid hitbox. A visibly successful hit is not rejected by hidden accuracy or evasion rolls. Blocking, immunity, invulnerability, and similar defenses remain possible when clearly communicated.

**Combat language:** [L] Projectile combat is the game's central combat language, but not every action must literally be a projectile. Clearly authored melee arcs, beams, ground effects, area attacks, and other class or enemy mechanics are allowed when they preserve direct control, free aiming where appropriate, and readable outcomes. The already-established weapon-defined primary attacks remain projectile-based.

**Intensity curve:** [L] Open-world combat is generally more relaxed and lower-density. Dungeons, world bosses, and raids become faster and increasingly bullet-hell-like. Endgame encounters may become extremely intense, provided attacks, hazards, safe spaces, and deaths remain readable, fair, and learnable.

**Pause:** [L] Single-player combat can normally be paused, freezing the action completely. Pausing is not used to issue commands or perform combat actions.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact universal movement and defensive actions, skill activation, ability-specific animation commitments, detailed hitbox and collision geometry, aim assistance, and optional focus-target behaviour remain unresolved for CORE-33 onward.

**CORE-32. What does the primary attack do, and why is it enjoyable without rewards?**

*Consider: Define hold-to-fire behavior, aiming demand, weapon identity, hit feedback, and positioning.*

**Answer/status:** [L/P] The primary attack is a continuously available, freely aimed, weapon-defined projectile action built around dependable controls, deterministic patterns, positioning, terrain, and readable impact feedback.

**Input and firing:** [L] Tapping LMB performs one primary attack. Holding LMB attacks continuously at the equipped weapon's cadence. Repeated clicking is never required.

**Autofire:** [L/P] A remappable autofire hotkey toggles the same held-attack state on or off. While enabled, attacks follow the player's current mouse or controller aim—even into empty space. Autofire never selects, tracks, or aims at enemies automatically, so it remains an input convenience rather than passive auto-combat. Its state has a clear HUD indicator.

**Resource rule:** [L] Ordinary primary attacks consume no mana, stamina, ammunition, durability, or reload resource. They are always available; mana is reserved for active abilities.

**Movement and consistency:** [L] Movement, aiming, and attacking remain independent. Ordinary primary attacks do not unexpectedly root, slow, direction-lock, displace, or reduce aim responsiveness. There are no hidden attack slowdowns, random recoil movements, or animation locks. This consistency allows the player to develop dependable dodge and aim instincts.

**Authored exceptions:** [L/P] Special weapons may use clearly communicated charge times, wind-ups, recoil, or movement trade-offs as part of their identity. These are deliberate exceptions rather than the baseline and must behave identically under identical conditions.

**Deterministic patterns:** [L] Primary attacks use deterministic, authored firing patterns. There is no random accuracy bloom or unexplained projectile drift. Fixed spreads, fans, bursts, and other formations are allowed, but identical inputs under identical conditions produce identical shot placement.

**Weapon identity:** [L/P] The equipped weapon defines the complete primary-attack behaviour—not merely its damage. Projectile count, formation, range, speed, cadence, piercing, bouncing, returning, bursts, waves, orbiting behaviour, and other readable spatial properties can all contribute to weapon identity. Standard tiered weapons may preserve a familiar attack style while improving numerically; alternative and unique weapons may substantially change positioning, aiming, and combat rhythm.

**Development rule:** [P/T] Development should prototype a broad range of attack behaviours and combinations before narrowing the final arsenal. Experiments are not automatically guaranteed to ship. Every finished weapon must remain readable, learnable, useful, deterministic, and meaningfully distinct rather than becoming random mechanical clutter.

**Hit feedback:** [L/P] Ordinary hits combine clear projectile impacts, brief enemy flashes, responsive audio, and readable damage numbers that can be reduced or disabled. Kills, immunities, blocked damage, and any critical or heavy hits receive appropriately stronger feedback.

**Readability boundary:** [L] Routine attacks do not cause constant screen shake, movement disruption, or global hit-stop. Feedback must remain satisfying without obscuring dense encounters or interfering with precise dodging.

**Terrain and positioning:** [L/P] Ordinary projectiles require a clear line of fire and collide predictably with solid terrain. Collision should match visible geometry and avoid frustrating corner snagging. Clearly identified weapons may pierce, bounce from, curve around, or arc over obstacles as authored exceptions. Different weapon patterns should reward different positioning and attack angles.

**Distance rule:** [L] There is no universal hidden distance penalty applied to every weapon.

**No-reward enjoyment:** [L/P] Primary attacks are intended to remain enjoyable without rewards through dependable movement and aiming, continuous spatial decisions, learnable weapon behaviour, meaningful use of range, angles, and terrain, and responsive but readable impact feedback.

**Deferred detail:** [U] Exact weapon statistics, cadence ranges, individual patterns, exceptional mechanics, default autofire binding and HUD treatment, collision tuning, and feedback tuning remain unresolved for prototyping and later combat planning.

**CORE-42. How does a player deliberately acquire a desired important item?**

*Consider: Random direct drop, guaranteed tokens, crafting, selectable rewards, pity, duplicate conversion, or a combination.*

# Part XIV - Production, milestones, testing, and risk

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />

</table>

## B. Planning extraction sheet

**PLAN-01. Locked game promise**

Explore a vast fantasy world filled with battles and events as a permanently chosen Archer, Warrior, or Mage; master freely aimed projectile combat; gain levels and class skill points through quests, monsters, and other activities; and collect progressively stronger RPG equipment and class-specific weapons, including rare boss-dropped uniques whose powerful but situational projectile patterns create distinct ways to fight.

**PLAN-02. Locked design pillars**

1\. Freely aimed projectile combat is satisfying, readable, and expressive in its own right.

2\. Meaningful progression continually expands who the player is, what they can do, and what they can pursue.

3\. Exploration builds world knowledge, and world knowledge turns discovery into deliberate pursuits.

4\. Mastering major challenges becomes a rewarding long-term pursuit.

**PLAN-03. Locked anti-pillars and exclusions**

1\. No passive or self-playing combat.

2\. No MMO-style waiting used as content.

3\. No directionless or unnecessarily cluttered grind.

4\. No unreadable projectile combat; endgame intensity is welcome when it remains legible, learnable, and fair.

5\. No universally superior unique weapon.

**PLAN-04. Hard production constraints**

## C. Planning-readiness checklist

\[ \] The core promise and intended player are clear.

\[ \] The project constraints are honest and quantified.

\[ \] The first playable milestone can disprove the combat concept cheaply.

\[ \] Unknown combat decisions have explicit prototype tests.

\[ \] Class, weapon, skill, level, and equipment responsibilities do not overlap unnecessarily.

\[ \] Important loot has a finite acquisition path.

\[ \] The world structure supports choice without erasing progression.

\[ \] Repeated travel and routine labor have planned compression.

\[ \] The vertical slice proves a complete expedition, not only combat.

\[ \] Success criteria, risks, cut options, and milestone gates are written down.

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr class="header">
<th><p><strong>Recommended next use</strong></p>
<p>Complete CORE-01 through CORE-55 first. The remaining sections can be answered in the same order as implementation. A response marked [T] is not a failure to decide: it becomes a concrete prototype task in the roadmap.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
